Yeah… work, definitely. I mean, relationships maybe in the sense that people are getting tired of me being unreliable, but the big thing is work.

It’s started to affect how people see me, which is… hard to admit. I missed a patent filing checkpoint, and that was not a small thing. And then there was this architecture review that went pretty badly—not technically impossible, just messy, and I wasn’t as prepared or as clear as I should’ve been. Afterward I could tell collaborators were kind of wondering if I’m actually able to lead the larger project.

Which is frustrating, because I know the material. I can talk about the architecture for hours if we’re at a whiteboard, or—sorry, this is a tangent—but I actually like those deep technical debates when they’re contained. But when it’s formal and there are deadlines and everyone’s depending on me to pull it together, that’s where it starts falling apart. So yes, it’s interfering.

I think it goes way back, honestly. It’s not just a recent work-stress thing.

Looking back, even in grade school, teachers used to say I was bright but that I daydreamed a lot. I’d finish assignments and then somehow not turn them in, or I’d lose the folder with the homework in it. Which is funny—well, not funny, but I remember having these color-coded folders at one point because my mom was trying to help me keep track of things, and I’d still lose the one I needed.

So yeah, this was definitely happening long before I was a teenager. It’s just that now the consequences are bigger.

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, that’s actually probably the biggest thing right now.

What finally pushed me to come in is that I feel like I can’t organize all the moving parts anymore. I’ll have patent notes in one place, workshop prep half-started somewhere else, gallery deadlines—well, the gallery thing has its own email thread, and then team decisions are buried in Slack or meeting notes—and I end up just starting whatever feels loudest or most urgent in the moment instead of what actually matters.

So it’s not that I don’t care about deadlines. It’s more like everything is competing for attention and I can’t reliably sort it into a sane order.

I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, outside of work there’s less of a formal “deadline” structure, so it’s harder for me to tell what counts as organization versus just normal life messiness.

Like, I’ve got patent notes, workshop prep, gallery deadlines, team decisions—actually the gallery thing is a whole separate mess—but the main issue is I end up doing whatever is loudest or most urgent-feeling in the moment, not necessarily what actually matters. And then important stuff just sort of… sits there. So I guess it finally got to the point where I thought I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself struggling to focus on a single task for an extended period of time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I’ll sit down to code something and mean to stay with it, but if Slack pings, or a browser tab flashes, I’m just… gone. Or even if some visual idea pops into my head, like a layout or design thing, I’ll follow that instead.\n\nAnd then suddenly I’ve spent twenty minutes down some totally unrelated design rabbit hole and I have to come back like, “What was I even doing in this function?” It’s frustrating because it doesn’t always feel like I’m choosing to switch tasks—it just kind of happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It’s not like I’m bouncing off the walls exactly, but on long design calls I can feel this pressure kind of building in my body, like I need to get up. So I’ll pace around my office, or move from one chair to another, even while I’m trying to look like I’m paying attention.\n\nAnd sometimes I tell myself it’s just because the call is too long or the topic got too abstract—like when people start debating button spacing for twenty minutes—but, yeah, I guess the short answer is I have a hard time just sitting still.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with focus and restlessness interfering with your relationships or work performance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… work, definitely. I mean, relationships maybe in the sense that people are getting tired of me being unreliable, but the big thing is work.\n\nIt’s started to affect how people see me, which is… hard to admit. I missed a patent filing checkpoint, and that was not a small thing. And then there was this architecture review that went pretty badly—not technically impossible, just messy, and I wasn’t as prepared or as clear as I should’ve been. Afterward I could tell collaborators were kind of wondering if I’m actually able to lead the larger project.\n\nWhich is frustrating, because I know the material. I can talk about the architecture for hours if we’re at a whiteboard, or—sorry, this is a tangent—but I actually like those deep technical debates when they’re contained. But when it’s formal and there are deadlines and everyone’s depending on me to pull it together, that’s where it starts falling apart. So yes, it’s interfering.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty with focus and restlessness, or did these issues start more recently?\n\nPatient: I think it goes way back, honestly. It’s not just a recent work-stress thing.\n\nLooking back, even in grade school, teachers used to say I was bright but that I daydreamed a lot. I’d finish assignments and then somehow not turn them in, or I’d lose the folder with the homework in it. I’d have to think about it more.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I don’t think of myself as someone who cuts people off all the time, but conversations at work can get kind of fast, especially in technical discussions, and sometimes people are half-answering a question while I’m already thinking of the next piece of it.\n\nBut whether that’s “interrupting” in the way you mean… I don’t know. I’d probably have to ask other people. I’m aware I can jump in when I’m excited about an idea, but I don’t know how often that comes across as rude or disruptive.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble keeping track of tasks, appointments, or deadlines?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s actually probably the biggest thing right now.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in is that I feel like I can’t organize all the moving parts anymore. I’ll have patent notes in one place, workshop prep half-started somewhere else, gallery deadlines—well, the gallery thing has its own email thread, and then team decisions are buried in Slack or meeting notes—and I end up just starting whatever feels loudest or most urgent in the moment instead of what actually matters.\n\nSo it’s not that I don’t care about deadlines. It’s more like everything is competing for attention and I can’t reliably sort it into a sane order.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with organization and focus affecting your personal relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, outside of work there’s less of a formal “deadline” structure, so it’s harder for me to tell what counts as organization versus just normal life messiness.\n\nI don’t think people are, like, sitting me down and saying, “You’re impossible to deal with.” But I can imagine it probably shows up in little ways. Plans get discussed over text, then there’s a calendar invite, then someone mentions a different time in passing, and I’ll think I know what’s happening until I realize I’m remembering an older version of the plan.\n\nBut whether that’s affecting relationships in a major way… I honestly don’t know. I haven’t really tracked it like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’m usually blurting out answers before someone finishes, at least not in a way I’ve noticed.

Sometimes if I think I know where a question is going, especially in a meeting, I might start forming the answer before they’re done, but whether I actually say it out loud too early… I don’t know. It’s funny, because in software stuff people sometimes ask these very long setup questions, and halfway through I’m already thinking about the bug or the architecture issue, and then I have to remind myself to listen to the rest because there might be some detail that changes everything.

So, I guess maybe occasionally? But I haven’t had people complain about that specifically.

I can talk about the architecture for hours if we’re at a whiteboard, or—sorry, this is a tangent—but I actually like those deep technical debates when they’re contained. But when it’s formal and there are deadlines and everyone’s depending on me to pull it together, that’s where it starts falling apart. So yes, it’s interfering.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty with focus and restlessness, or did these issues start more recently?\n\nPatient: I think it goes way back, honestly. It’s not just a recent work-stress thing.\n\nLooking back, even in grade school, teachers used to say I was bright but that I daydreamed a lot. I’d finish assignments and then somehow not turn them in, or I’d lose the folder with the homework in it. Which is funny—well, not funny, but I remember having these color-coded folders at one point because my mom was trying to help me keep track of things, and I’d still lose the one I needed.\n\nSo yeah, this was definitely happening long before I was a teenager. It’s just that now the consequences are bigger.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, actually. I don’t think of myself as especially impulsive in the big, dramatic sense.\n\nI mean, I can make quick decisions sometimes, but… in software you sort of have to, or you end up in endless planning meetings, and then someone brings up edge cases from a product line that doesn’t even exist anymore—sorry, that’s probably not what you’re asking.\n\nIf you mean like doing risky things without caring what happens, I don’t really notice that as a pattern. I’d have to think about it more.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I don’t think of myself as someone who cuts people off all the time, but conversations at work can get kind of fast, especially in technical discussions, and sometimes people are half-answering a question while I’m already thinking of the next piece of it.\n\nBut whether that’s “interrupting” in the way you mean… I don’t know. I’d probably have to ask other people. I’m aware I can jump in when I’m excited about an idea, but I don’t know how often that comes across as rude or disruptive.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble keeping track of tasks, appointments, or deadlines?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s actually probably the biggest thing right now.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in is that I feel like I can’t organize all the moving parts anymore. I’ll have patent notes in one place, workshop prep half-started somewhere else, gallery deadlines—well, the gallery thing has its own email thread, and then team decisions are buried in Slack or meeting notes—and I end up just starting whatever feels loudest or most urgent in the moment instead of what actually matters.\n\nSo it’s not that I don’t care about deadlines. It’s more like everything is competing for attention and I can’t reliably sort it into a sane order.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with organization and focus affecting your personal relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, outside of work there’s less of a formal “deadline” structure, so it’s harder for me to tell what counts as organization versus just normal life messiness.\n\nI don’t think people are, like, sitting me down and saying, “You’re impossible to deal with.” But I can imagine it probably shows up in little ways. Plans get discussed over text, then there’s a calendar invite, then someone mentions a different time in passing, and I’ll think I know what’s happening until I realize I’m remembering an older version of the plan.\n\nBut whether that’s affecting relationships in a major way… I honestly don’t know. I haven’t really tracked it like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you blurt out answers before questions have been completed?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’m usually blurting out answers before someone finishes, at least not in a way I’ve noticed.\n\nSometimes if I think I know where a question is going, especially in a meeting, I might start forming the answer before they’re done, but whether I actually say it out loud too early… I don’t know. It’s funny, because in software stuff people sometimes ask these very long setup questions, and halfway through I’m already thinking about the bug or the architecture issue, and then I have to remind myself to listen to the rest because there might be some detail that changes everything.\n\nSo, I guess maybe occasionally? Which is funny—well, not funny, but I remember having these color-coded folders at one point because my mom was trying to help me keep track of things, and I’d still lose the one I needed.\n\nSo yeah, this was definitely happening long before I was a teenager. It’s just that now the consequences are bigger.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, actually. I don’t think of myself as especially impulsive in the big, dramatic sense.\n\nI mean, I can make quick decisions sometimes, but… in software you sort of have to, or you end up in endless planning meetings, and then someone brings up edge cases from a product line that doesn’t even exist anymore—sorry, that’s probably not what you’re asking.\n\nIf you mean like doing risky things without caring what happens, I don’t really notice that as a pattern. I’d have to think about it more.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I don’t think of myself as someone who cuts people off all the time, but conversations at work can get kind of fast, especially in technical discussions, and sometimes people are half-answering a question while I’m already thinking of the next piece of it.\n\nBut whether that’s “interrupting” in the way you mean… I don’t know. I’d probably have to ask other people. I’m aware I can jump in when I’m excited about an idea, but I don’t know how often that comes across as rude or disruptive.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble keeping track of tasks, appointments, or deadlines?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s actually probably the biggest thing right now.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in is that I feel like I can’t organize all the moving parts anymore. I’ll have patent notes in one place, workshop prep half-started somewhere else, gallery deadlines—well, the gallery thing has its own email thread, and then team decisions are buried in Slack or meeting notes—and I end up just starting whatever feels loudest or most urgent in the moment instead of what actually matters.\n\nSo it’s not that I don’t care about deadlines. It’s more like everything is competing for attention and I can’t reliably sort it into a sane order.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with organization and focus affecting your personal relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, outside of work there’s less of a formal “deadline” structure, so it’s harder for me to tell what counts as organization versus just normal life messiness.\n\nI don’t think people are, like, sitting me down and saying, “You’re impossible to deal with.” But I can imagine it probably shows up in little ways. Plans get discussed over text, then there’s a calendar invite, then someone mentions a different time in passing, and I’ll think I know what’s happening until I realize I’m remembering an older version of the plan.\n\nBut whether that’s affecting relationships in a major way… I honestly don’t know. I haven’t really tracked it like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you blurt out answers before questions have been completed?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’m usually blurting out answers before someone finishes, at least not in a way I’ve noticed.\n\nSometimes if I think I know where a question is going, especially in a meeting, I might start forming the answer before they’re done, but whether I actually say it out loud too early… I don’t know. It’s funny, because in software stuff people sometimes ask these very long setup questions, and halfway through I’m already thinking about the bug or the architecture issue, and then I have to remind myself to listen to the rest because there might be some detail that changes everything.\n\nSo, I guess maybe occasionally? But I haven’t had people complain about that specifically.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself feeling overwhelmed by your emotions?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way. “Overwhelmed by emotions” makes me think of, like, suddenly crying or exploding or something, and that’s not really how I think of myself.\n\nI do get overwhelmed in general, but it’s usually more like there are too many inputs and I sort of mentally jam up. I’m trying to think of a clean example, and nothing obvious is coming to mind.

I mean, “impulse” makes me think of big dramatic things, like quitting a job on the spot or buying a car without checking your bank account, and I’m not really that person. I’m more likely to overthink things, honestly.

There are probably small moments where I respond too quickly to something or agree to something before I’ve really looked at my schedule, but then I’m not sure if that counts as impulse or just being distracted in the moment. Like with software, sometimes you make a change thinking, “Oh, this is definitely the issue,” and then later realize there was some edge case you didn’t consider—but that’s not really a life consequence thing, it’s more just how debugging goes.

So… I guess I don’t have a strong example. I haven’t noticed myself acting impulsively in a way that caused major problems.

And then there was this architecture review that went pretty badly—not technically impossible, just messy, and I wasn’t as prepared or as clear as I should’ve been. Afterward I could tell collaborators were kind of wondering if I’m actually able to lead the larger project.\n\nWhich is frustrating, because I know the material. I can talk about the architecture for hours if we’re at a whiteboard, or—sorry, this is a tangent—but I actually like those deep technical debates when they’re contained. But when it’s formal and there are deadlines and everyone’s depending on me to pull it together, that’s where it starts falling apart. So yes, it’s interfering.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty with focus and restlessness, or did these issues start more recently?\n\nPatient: I think it goes way back, honestly. It’s not just a recent work-stress thing.\n\nLooking back, even in grade school, teachers used to say I was bright but that I daydreamed a lot. I’d finish assignments and then somehow not turn them in, or I’d lose the folder with the homework in it. Which is funny—well, not funny, but I remember having these color-coded folders at one point because my mom was trying to help me keep track of things, and I’d still lose the one I needed.\n\nSo yeah, this was definitely happening long before I was a teenager. It’s just that now the consequences are bigger.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, actually. I don’t think of myself as especially impulsive in the big, dramatic sense.\n\nI mean, I can make quick decisions sometimes, but… in software you sort of have to, or you end up in endless planning meetings, and then someone brings up edge cases from a product line that doesn’t even exist anymore—sorry, that’s probably not what you’re asking.\n\nIf you mean like doing risky things without caring what happens, I don’t really notice that as a pattern. I’d have to think about it more.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I don’t think of myself as someone who cuts people off all the time, but conversations at work can get kind of fast, especially in technical discussions, and sometimes people are half-answering a question while I’m already thinking of the next piece of it.\n\nBut whether that’s “interrupting” in the way you mean… I don’t know. I’d probably have to ask other people. I’m aware I can jump in when I’m excited about an idea, but I don’t know how often that comes across as rude or disruptive.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble keeping track of tasks, appointments, or deadlines?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s actually probably the biggest thing right now.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in is that I feel like I can’t organize all the moving parts anymore. I’ll have patent notes in one place, workshop prep half-started somewhere else, gallery deadlines—well, the gallery thing has its own email thread, and then team decisions are buried in Slack or meeting notes—and I end up just starting whatever feels loudest or most urgent in the moment instead of what actually matters.\n\nSo it’s not that I don’t care about deadlines. It’s more like everything is competing for attention and I can’t reliably sort it into a sane order.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with organization and focus affecting your personal relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, outside of work there’s less of a formal “deadline” structure, so it’s harder for me to tell what counts as organization versus just normal life messiness.\n\nI don’t think people are, like, sitting me down and saying, “You’re impossible to deal with.” But I can imagine it probably shows up in little ways. Plans get discussed over text, then there’s a calendar invite, then someone mentions a different time in passing, and I’ll think I know what’s happening until I realize I’m remembering an older version of the plan.\n\nBut whether that’s affecting relationships in a major way… I honestly don’t know. I haven’t really tracked it like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you blurt out answers before questions have been completed?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’m usually blurting out answers before someone finishes, at least not in a way I’ve noticed.\n\nSometimes if I think I know where a question is going, especially in a meeting, I might start forming the answer before they’re done, but whether I actually say it out loud too early… I don’t know. It’s funny, because in software stuff people sometimes ask these very long setup questions, and halfway through I’m already thinking about the bug or the architecture issue, and then I have to remind myself to listen to the rest because there might be some detail that changes everything.\n\nSo, I guess maybe occasionally? But I haven’t had people complain about that specifically.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself feeling overwhelmed by your emotions?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way. “Overwhelmed by emotions” makes me think of, like, suddenly crying or exploding or something, and that’s not really how I think of myself.\n\nI do get overwhelmed in general, but it’s usually more like there are too many inputs and I sort of mentally jam up. Emotionally… I don’t know. I can get frustrated, sure, especially when I feel like I’m behind, but I’m not sure if that’s an emotion thing or just a normal reaction.\n\nSorry, I’m kind of answering a different question. I guess my honest answer is: I haven’t really noticed that as a major pattern.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any times when you acted on impulse without fully thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a clean example, and nothing obvious is coming to mind.\n\nI mean, “impulse” makes me think of big dramatic things, like quitting a job on the spot or buying a car without checking your bank account, and I’m not really that person. I’m more likely to overthink things, honestly.\n\nThere are probably small moments where I respond too quickly to something or agree to something before I’ve really looked at my schedule, but then I’m not sure if that counts as impulse or just being distracted in the moment. Like with software, sometimes you make a change thinking, “Oh, this is definitely the issue,” and then later realize there was some edge case you didn’t consider—but that’s not really a life consequence thing, it’s more just how debugging goes.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t have a strong example. I haven’t noticed myself acting impulsively in a way that caused major problems.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Yeah, I think so. Looking back, it wasn’t just later on. In grade school there were comments like, you know, “bright kid, but daydreams,” that kind of thing.

I also remember finishing assignments and then somehow not turning them in, which sounds ridiculous now because the hard part was already done. And homework folders—I lost those a lot, long before I was a teenager. I guess at the time it was treated as me being careless or not organized, but when I look back, that pattern was already there pretty early.

It’s not like I’m bouncing off the walls exactly, but on long design calls I can feel this pressure kind of building in my body, like I need to get up. So I’ll pace around my office, or move from one chair to another, even while I’m trying to look like I’m paying attention.\n\nAnd sometimes I tell myself it’s just because the call is too long or the topic got too abstract—like when people start debating button spacing for twenty minutes—but, yeah, I guess the short answer is I have a hard time just sitting still.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with focus and restlessness interfering with your relationships or work performance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… work, definitely. I missed a patent filing checkpoint, and that was not a small thing. And then there was this architecture review that went pretty badly—not technically impossible, just messy, and I wasn’t as prepared or as clear as I should’ve been. Afterward I could tell collaborators were kind of wondering if I’m actually able to lead the larger project.\n\nWhich is frustrating, because I know the material. I can talk about the architecture for hours if we’re at a whiteboard, or—sorry, this is a tangent—but I actually like those deep technical debates when they’re contained. But when it’s formal and there are deadlines and everyone’s depending on me to pull it together, that’s where it starts falling apart. So yes, it’s interfering.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty with focus and restlessness, or did these issues start more recently?\n\nPatient: I think it goes way back, honestly. It’s not just a recent work-stress thing.\n\nLooking back, even in grade school, teachers used to say I was bright but that I daydreamed a lot. I’d finish assignments and then somehow not turn them in, or I’d lose the folder with the homework in it. Which is funny—well, not funny, but I remember having these color-coded folders at one point because my mom was trying to help me keep track of things, and I’d still lose the one I needed.\n\nSo yeah, this was definitely happening long before I was a teenager. It’s just that now the consequences are bigger.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, actually. I don’t think of myself as especially impulsive in the big, dramatic sense.\n\nI mean, I can make quick decisions sometimes, but… in software you sort of have to, or you end up in endless planning meetings, and then someone brings up edge cases from a product line that doesn’t even exist anymore—sorry, that’s probably not what you’re asking.\n\nIf you mean like doing risky things without caring what happens, I don’t really notice that as a pattern. I’d have to think about it more.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I’d probably have to ask other people. I’m aware I can jump in when I’m excited about an idea, but I don’t know how often that comes across as rude or disruptive.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble keeping track of tasks, appointments, or deadlines?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s actually probably the biggest thing right now.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in is that I feel like I can’t organize all the moving parts anymore. It’s more like everything is competing for attention and I can’t reliably sort it into a sane order.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with organization and focus affecting your personal relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, outside of work there’s less of a formal “deadline” structure, so it’s harder for me to tell what counts as organization versus just normal life messiness.\n\nI don’t think people are, like, sitting me down and saying, “You’re impossible to deal with.” But I can imagine it probably shows up in little ways. Plans get discussed over text, then there’s a calendar invite, then someone mentions a different time in passing, and I’ll think I know what’s happening until I realize I’m remembering an older version of the plan.\n\nBut whether that’s affecting relationships in a major way… I honestly don’t know. I haven’t really tracked it like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you blurt out answers before questions have been completed?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I’m more likely to overthink things, honestly.\n\nThere are probably small moments where I respond too quickly to something or agree to something before I’ve really looked at my schedule, but then I’m not sure if that counts as impulse or just being distracted in the moment. Like with software, sometimes you make a change thinking, “Oh, this is definitely the issue,” and then later realize there was some edge case you didn’t consider—but that’s not really a life consequence thing, it’s more just how debugging goes.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t have a strong example. I haven’t noticed myself acting impulsively in a way that caused major problems.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience difficulties with focus, restlessness, or impulsivity as a child, aside from what you mentioned about daydreaming and losing folders?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Looking back, it wasn’t just later on. In grade school there were comments like, you know, “bright kid, but daydreams,” that kind of thing.\n\nI also remember finishing assignments and then somehow not turning them in, which sounds ridiculous now because the hard part was already done. And homework folders—I lost those a lot, long before I was a teenager. I guess at the time it was treated as me being careless or not organized, but when I look back, that pattern was already there pretty early.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often avoid or dislike tasks that require sustained mental effort?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 46 / software_developer / graduate\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I don’t think I avoid “mental effort” in the sense that I’m lazy about thinking—my job is basically thinking all day, or at least pretending to while staring at logs and wondering why something broke.

But sustained effort… yeah, maybe that depends on the task. If it’s something where I can get into it, I can stay with it. If it’s something kind of open-ended or tedious, I’ll sometimes circle around it for a while before actually starting. Though I’m realizing I’m talking more generally than answering your question.

So… I guess I don’t have a clean yes-or-no. I know some tasks feel harder to start and stick with, but I’m not sure I’d say I often avoid them across the board.

So yes, it’s interfering.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty with focus and restlessness, or did these issues start more recently?\n\nPatient: I think it goes way back, honestly. I’d have to think about it more.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I’d probably have to ask other people. I’m aware I can jump in when I’m excited about an idea, but I don’t know how often that comes across as rude or disruptive.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble keeping track of tasks, appointments, or deadlines?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s actually probably the biggest thing right now.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in is that I feel like I can’t organize all the moving parts anymore. It’s more like everything is competing for attention and I can’t reliably sort it into a sane order.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with organization and focus affecting your personal relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. Like with software, sometimes you make a change thinking, “Oh, this is definitely the issue,” and then later realize there was some edge case you didn’t consider—but that’s not really a life consequence thing, it’s more just how debugging goes.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t have a strong example. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I can give a clean answer to that. “Follow through” can mean a few different things, right? Like, if someone gives me a set of instructions at work, I might do the main thing and then realize later there was some smaller detail buried in there that I didn’t account for—but that also happens because software instructions are often, you know, half a sentence in a ticket written by someone who assumes you already know the entire backstory.

Finishing tasks I’m interested in… I mean, sometimes I do finish them, especially if there’s a clear endpoint. But if it sort of sprawls, or if the “done” part is fuzzy, then I can drift into improving some side piece instead. Actually that reminds me of when you start cleaning one room and end up reorganizing a drawer, except I’m not saying I’m constantly doing that, it’s just the kind of thing I mean.

So… I guess I’ve noticed it in some situations, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s consistently hard, or if it’s just normal task-management stuff. I’d probably need you to define what you mean by “hard to follow through.”

So yes, it’s interfering.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty with focus and restlessness, or did these issues start more recently?\n\nPatient: I think it goes way back, honestly. It’s just that now the consequences are bigger.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, actually. I’d have to think about it more.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I don’t think of myself as someone who cuts people off all the time, but conversations at work can get kind of fast, especially in technical discussions, and sometimes people are half-answering a question while I’m already thinking of the next piece of it.\n\nBut whether that’s “interrupting” in the way you mean… I don’t know. I’d probably have to ask other people. I’m aware I can jump in when I’m excited about an idea, but I don’t know how often that comes across as rude or disruptive.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble keeping track of tasks, appointments, or deadlines?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s actually probably the biggest thing right now.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in is that I feel like I can’t organize all the moving parts anymore. I’ll have patent notes in one place, workshop prep half-started somewhere else, gallery deadlines—well, the gallery thing has its own email thread, and then team decisions are buried in Slack or meeting notes—and I end up just starting whatever feels loudest or most urgent in the moment instead of what actually matters.\n\nSo it’s not that I don’t care about deadlines. It’s more like everything is competing for attention and I can’t reliably sort it into a sane order.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with organization and focus affecting your personal relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, outside of work there’s less of a formal “deadline” structure, so it’s harder for me to tell what counts as organization versus just normal life messiness.\n\nI don’t think people are, like, sitting me down and saying, “You’re impossible to deal with.” But I can imagine it probably shows up in little ways. Plans get discussed over text, then there’s a calendar invite, then someone mentions a different time in passing, and I’ll think I know what’s happening until I realize I’m remembering an older version of the plan.\n\nBut whether that’s affecting relationships in a major way… I honestly don’t know. I haven’t really tracked it like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you blurt out answers before questions have been completed?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’m usually blurting out answers before someone finishes, at least not in a way I’ve noticed.\n\nSometimes if I think I know where a question is going, especially in a meeting, I might start forming the answer before they’re done, but whether I actually say it out loud too early… I don’t know. It’s funny, because in software stuff people sometimes ask these very long setup questions, and halfway through I’m already thinking about the bug or the architecture issue, and then I have to remind myself to listen to the rest because there might be some detail that changes everything.\n\nSo, I guess maybe occasionally? I mean, outside of work there’s less of a formal “deadline” structure, so it’s harder for me to tell what counts as organization versus just normal life messiness.\n\nI don’t think people are, like, sitting me down and saying, “You’re impossible to deal with.” But I can imagine it probably shows up in little ways. Plans get discussed over text, then there’s a calendar invite, then someone mentions a different time in passing, and I’ll think I know what’s happening until I realize I’m remembering an older version of the plan.\n\nBut whether that’s affecting relationships in a major way… I honestly don’t know. I haven’t really tracked it like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you blurt out answers before questions have been completed?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’m usually blurting out answers before someone finishes, at least not in a way I’ve noticed.\n\nSometimes if I think I know where a question is going, especially in a meeting, I might start forming the answer before they’re done, but whether I actually say it out loud too early… I don’t know. It’s funny, because in software stuff people sometimes ask these very long setup questions, and halfway through I’m already thinking about the bug or the architecture issue, and then I have to remind myself to listen to the rest because there might be some detail that changes everything.\n\nSo, I guess maybe occasionally? But I haven’t had people complain about that specifically.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself feeling overwhelmed by your emotions?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way. “Overwhelmed by emotions” makes me think of, like, suddenly crying or exploding or something, and that’s not really how I think of myself.\n\nI do get overwhelmed in general, but it’s usually more like there are too many inputs and I sort of mentally jam up. Emotionally… I don’t know. I can get frustrated, sure, especially when I feel like I’m behind, but I’m not sure if that’s an emotion thing or just a normal reaction.\n\nSorry, I’m kind of answering a different question. I guess my honest answer is: I haven’t really noticed that as a major pattern.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any times when you acted on impulse without fully thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a clean example, and nothing obvious is coming to mind.\n\nI mean, “impulse” makes me think of big dramatic things, like quitting a job on the spot or buying a car without checking your bank account, and I’m not really that person. I’m more likely to overthink things, honestly.\n\nThere are probably small moments where I respond too quickly to something or agree to something before I’ve really looked at my schedule, but then I’m not sure if that counts as impulse or just being distracted in the moment. Like with software, sometimes you make a change thinking, “Oh, this is definitely the issue,” and then later realize there was some edge case you didn’t consider—but that’s not really a life consequence thing, it’s more just how debugging goes.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t have a strong example. I haven’t noticed myself acting impulsively in a way that caused major problems.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience difficulties with focus, restlessness, or impulsivity as a child, aside from what you mentioned about daydreaming and losing folders?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Looking back, it wasn’t just later on. In grade school there were comments like, you know, “bright kid, but daydreams,” that kind of thing.\n\nI also remember finishing assignments and then somehow not turning them in, which sounds ridiculous now because the hard part was already done. And homework folders—I lost those a lot, long before I was a teenager. I guess at the time it was treated as me being careless or not organized, but when I look back, that pattern was already there pretty early.\n\nDoctor: Do you often avoid or dislike tasks that require sustained mental effort?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. If it’s something where I can get into it, I can stay with it. If it’s something kind of open-ended or tedious, I’ll sometimes circle around it for a while before actually starting. Though I’m realizing I’m talking more generally than answering your question.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t have a clean yes-or-no. I know some tasks feel harder to start and stick with, but I’m not sure I’d say I often avoid them across the board.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to follow through on instructions or finish tasks, even when you're interested in them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give a clean answer to that. “Follow through” can mean a few different things, right? I don’t think so? I mean, I might jump in if I think I know where someone is going, but I feel like that’s kind of normal conversation too, depending on the person.

Actually, with software people, conversations are weird because everyone interrupts everyone on calls, and then there’s that delay where two people start talking and both stop, and then both start again. So it’s hard to tell what’s me versus just… Zoom being awful.

But socially, like with friends or family, I’m not aware of constantly blurting things out. I may have done it sometimes, sure, but I don’t think it’s something people complain about. At least not that I can think of.

I’d have to think about it more.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I don’t think of myself as someone who cuts people off all the time, but conversations at work can get kind of fast, especially in technical discussions, and sometimes people are half-answering a question while I’m already thinking of the next piece of it.\n\nBut whether that’s “interrupting” in the way you mean… I don’t know. I’d probably have to ask other people. I’m aware I can jump in when I’m excited about an idea, but I don’t know how often that comes across as rude or disruptive.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble keeping track of tasks, appointments, or deadlines?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s actually probably the biggest thing right now.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in is that I feel like I can’t organize all the moving parts anymore. I’ll have patent notes in one place, workshop prep half-started somewhere else, gallery deadlines—well, the gallery thing has its own email thread, and then team decisions are buried in Slack or meeting notes—and I end up just starting whatever feels loudest or most urgent in the moment instead of what actually matters.\n\nSo it’s not that I don’t care about deadlines. It’s more like everything is competing for attention and I can’t reliably sort it into a sane order.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with organization and focus affecting your personal relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, outside of work there’s less of a formal “deadline” structure, so it’s harder for me to tell what counts as organization versus just normal life messiness.\n\nI don’t think people are, like, sitting me down and saying, “You’re impossible to deal with.” But I can imagine it probably shows up in little ways. Plans get discussed over text, then there’s a calendar invite, then someone mentions a different time in passing, and I’ll think I know what’s happening until I realize I’m remembering an older version of the plan.\n\nBut whether that’s affecting relationships in a major way… I honestly don’t know. I haven’t really tracked it like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you blurt out answers before questions have been completed?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’m usually blurting out answers before someone finishes, at least not in a way I’ve noticed.\n\nSometimes if I think I know where a question is going, especially in a meeting, I might start forming the answer before they’re done, but whether I actually say it out loud too early… I don’t know. It’s funny, because in software stuff people sometimes ask these very long setup questions, and halfway through I’m already thinking about the bug or the architecture issue, and then I have to remind myself to listen to the rest because there might be some detail that changes everything.\n\nSo, I guess maybe occasionally? But I haven’t had people complain about that specifically.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself feeling overwhelmed by your emotions?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way. “Overwhelmed by emotions” makes me think of, like, suddenly crying or exploding or something, and that’s not really how I think of myself.\n\nI do get overwhelmed in general, but it’s usually more like there are too many inputs and I sort of mentally jam up. Emotionally… I don’t know. I can get frustrated, sure, especially when I feel like I’m behind, but I’m not sure if that’s an emotion thing or just a normal reaction.\n\nSorry, I’m kind of answering a different question. I guess my honest answer is: I haven’t really noticed that as a major pattern.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any times when you acted on impulse without fully thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a clean example, and nothing obvious is coming to mind.\n\nI mean, “impulse” makes me think of big dramatic things, like quitting a job on the spot or buying a car without checking your bank account, and I’m not really that person. I’m more likely to overthink things, honestly.\n\nThere are probably small moments where I respond too quickly to something or agree to something before I’ve really looked at my schedule, but then I’m not sure if that counts as impulse or just being distracted in the moment. Like with software, sometimes you make a change thinking, “Oh, this is definitely the issue,” and then later realize there was some edge case you didn’t consider—but that’s not really a life consequence thing, it’s more just how debugging goes.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t have a strong example. I haven’t noticed myself acting impulsively in a way that caused major problems.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience difficulties with focus, restlessness, or impulsivity as a child, aside from what you mentioned about daydreaming and losing folders?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Looking back, it wasn’t just later on. And homework folders—I lost those a lot, long before I was a teenager. I guess at the time it was treated as me being careless or not organized, but when I look back, that pattern was already there pretty early.\n\nDoctor: Do you often avoid or dislike tasks that require sustained mental effort?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. If it’s something where I can get into it, I can stay with it. If it’s something kind of open-ended or tedious, I’ll sometimes circle around it for a while before actually starting. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure. I don’t think I usually do big things without thinking about what might happen. Like, I’m not the kind of person who suddenly quits a job or buys a motorcycle or whatever without thinking it through.

Small things, maybe? But then I start wondering what counts as “considering consequences.” Like, if I reply to an email too quickly and later think, “Eh, I should’ve phrased that differently,” is that the same thing? Or is it more like dangerous decisions? Because those feel pretty different.

I guess I’d say I haven’t noticed it as a major pattern. But I may be defining it too narrowly.

I’m aware I can jump in when I’m excited about an idea, but I don’t know how often that comes across as rude or disruptive.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble keeping track of tasks, appointments, or deadlines?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s actually probably the biggest thing right now.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in is that I feel like I can’t organize all the moving parts anymore. I’ll have patent notes in one place, workshop prep half-started somewhere else, gallery deadlines—well, the gallery thing has its own email thread, and then team decisions are buried in Slack or meeting notes—and I end up just starting whatever feels loudest or most urgent in the moment instead of what actually matters.\n\nSo it’s not that I don’t care about deadlines. It’s more like everything is competing for attention and I can’t reliably sort it into a sane order.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with organization and focus affecting your personal relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, outside of work there’s less of a formal “deadline” structure, so it’s harder for me to tell what counts as organization versus just normal life messiness.\n\nI don’t think people are, like, sitting me down and saying, “You’re impossible to deal with.” But I can imagine it probably shows up in little ways. Plans get discussed over text, then there’s a calendar invite, then someone mentions a different time in passing, and I’ll think I know what’s happening until I realize I’m remembering an older version of the plan.\n\nBut whether that’s affecting relationships in a major way… I honestly don’t know. I haven’t really tracked it like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you blurt out answers before questions have been completed?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’m usually blurting out answers before someone finishes, at least not in a way I’ve noticed.\n\nSometimes if I think I know where a question is going, especially in a meeting, I might start forming the answer before they’re done, but whether I actually say it out loud too early… I don’t know. It’s funny, because in software stuff people sometimes ask these very long setup questions, and halfway through I’m already thinking about the bug or the architecture issue, and then I have to remind myself to listen to the rest because there might be some detail that changes everything.\n\nSo, I guess maybe occasionally? But I haven’t had people complain about that specifically.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself feeling overwhelmed by your emotions?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way. “Overwhelmed by emotions” makes me think of, like, suddenly crying or exploding or something, and that’s not really how I think of myself.\n\nI do get overwhelmed in general, but it’s usually more like there are too many inputs and I sort of mentally jam up. Emotionally… I don’t know. I’m more likely to overthink things, honestly.\n\nThere are probably small moments where I respond too quickly to something or agree to something before I’ve really looked at my schedule, but then I’m not sure if that counts as impulse or just being distracted in the moment. Like with software, sometimes you make a change thinking, “Oh, this is definitely the issue,” and then later realize there was some edge case you didn’t consider—but that’s not really a life consequence thing, it’s more just how debugging goes.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t have a strong example. I haven’t noticed myself acting impulsively in a way that caused major problems.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience difficulties with focus, restlessness, or impulsivity as a child, aside from what you mentioned about daydreaming and losing folders?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I don’t think of myself as especially impulsive in the big, dramatic sense.\n\nI mean, I can make quick decisions sometimes, but… in software you sort of have to, or you end up in endless planning meetings, and then someone brings up edge cases from a product line that doesn’t even exist anymore—sorry, that’s probably not what you’re asking.\n\nIf you mean like doing risky things without caring what happens, I don’t really notice that as a pattern. I’d have to think about it more.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I don’t think of myself as someone who cuts people off all the time, but conversations at work can get kind of fast, especially in technical discussions, and sometimes people are half-answering a question while I’m already thinking of the next piece of it.\n\nBut whether that’s “interrupting” in the way you mean… I don’t know. I’d probably have to ask other people. I’m aware I can jump in when I’m excited about an idea, but I don’t know how often that comes across as rude or disruptive.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble keeping track of tasks, appointments, or deadlines?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s actually probably the biggest thing right now.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in is that I feel like I can’t organize all the moving parts anymore. I’ll have patent notes in one place, workshop prep half-started somewhere else, gallery deadlines—well, the gallery thing has its own email thread, and then team decisions are buried in Slack or meeting notes—and I end up just starting whatever feels loudest or most urgent in the moment instead of what actually matters.\n\nSo it’s not that I don’t care about deadlines. It’s more like everything is competing for attention and I can’t reliably sort it into a sane order.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with organization and focus affecting your personal relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, outside of work there’s less of a formal “deadline” structure, so it’s harder for me to tell what counts as organization versus just normal life messiness.\n\nI don’t think people are, like, sitting me down and saying, “You’re impossible to deal with.” But I can imagine it probably shows up in little ways. Plans get discussed over text, then there’s a calendar invite, then someone mentions a different time in passing, and I’ll think I know what’s happening until I realize I’m remembering an older version of the plan.\n\nBut whether that’s affecting relationships in a major way… I honestly don’t know. I haven’t really tracked it like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you blurt out answers before questions have been completed?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’m usually blurting out answers before someone finishes, at least not in a way I’ve noticed.\n\nSometimes if I think I know where a question is going, especially in a meeting, I might start forming the answer before they’re done, but whether I actually say it out loud too early… I don’t know. It’s funny, because in software stuff people sometimes ask these very long setup questions, and halfway through I’m already thinking about the bug or the architecture issue, and then I have to remind myself to listen to the rest because there might be some detail that changes everything.\n\nSo, I guess maybe occasionally? But I haven’t had people complain about that specifically.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself feeling overwhelmed by your emotions?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way. “Overwhelmed by emotions” makes me think of, like, suddenly crying or exploding or something, and that’s not really how I think of myself.\n\nI do get overwhelmed in general, but it’s usually more like there are too many inputs and I sort of mentally jam up. Emotionally… I don’t know. I can get frustrated, sure, especially when I feel like I’m behind, but I’m not sure if that’s an emotion thing or just a normal reaction.\n\nSorry, I’m kind of answering a different question. I guess my honest answer is: I haven’t really noticed that as a major pattern.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any times when you acted on impulse without fully thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a clean example, and nothing obvious is coming to mind.\n\nI mean, “impulse” makes me think of big dramatic things, like quitting a job on the spot or buying a car without checking your bank account, and I’m not really that person. I’m more likely to overthink things, honestly.\n\nThere are probably small moments where I respond too quickly to something or agree to something before I’ve really looked at my schedule, but then I’m not sure if that counts as impulse or just being distracted in the moment. Like with software, sometimes you make a change thinking, “Oh, this is definitely the issue,” and then later realize there was some edge case you didn’t consider—but that’s not really a life consequence thing, it’s more just how debugging goes.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t have a strong example. I haven’t noticed myself acting impulsively in a way that caused major problems.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience difficulties with focus, restlessness, or impulsivity as a child, aside from what you mentioned about daydreaming and losing folders?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Looking back, it wasn’t just later on. In grade school there were comments like, you know, “bright kid, but daydreams,” that kind of thing.\n\nI also remember finishing assignments and then somehow not turning them in, which sounds ridiculous now because the hard part was already done. And homework folders—I lost those a lot, long before I was a teenager. I guess at the time it was treated as me being careless or not organized, but when I look back, that pattern was already there pretty early.\n\nDoctor: Do you often avoid or dislike tasks that require sustained mental effort?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I don’t think I avoid “mental effort” in the sense that I’m lazy about thinking—my job is basically thinking all day, or at least pretending to while staring at logs and wondering why something broke.\n\nBut sustained effort… yeah, maybe that depends on the task. If it’s something where I can get into it, I can stay with it. If it’s something kind of open-ended or tedious, I’ll sometimes circle around it for a while before actually starting. Though I’m realizing I’m talking more generally than answering your question.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t have a clean yes-or-no. I know some tasks feel harder to start and stick with, but I’m not sure I’d say I often avoid them across the board.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to follow through on instructions or finish tasks, even when you're interested in them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give a clean answer to that. “Follow through” can mean a few different things, right? Like, if someone gives me a set of instructions at work, I might do the main thing and then realize later there was some smaller detail buried in there that I didn’t account for—but that also happens because software instructions are often, you know, half a sentence in a ticket written by someone who assumes you already know the entire backstory.\n\nFinishing tasks I’m interested in… I mean, sometimes I do finish them, especially if there’s a clear endpoint. It’s more like everything is competing for attention and I can’t reliably sort it into a sane order.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with organization and focus affecting your personal relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, outside of work there’s less of a formal “deadline” structure, so it’s harder for me to tell what counts as organization versus just normal life messiness.\n\nI don’t think people are, like, sitting me down and saying, “You’re impossible to deal with.” But I can imagine it probably shows up in little ways. Plans get discussed over text, then there’s a calendar invite, then someone mentions a different time in passing, and I’ll think I know what’s happening until I realize I’m remembering an older version of the plan.\n\nBut whether that’s affecting relationships in a major way… I honestly don’t know. I haven’t really tracked it like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you blurt out answers before questions have been completed?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’m usually blurting out answers before someone finishes, at least not in a way I’ve noticed.\n\nSometimes if I think I know where a question is going, especially in a meeting, I might start forming the answer before they’re done, but whether I actually say it out loud too early… I don’t know. It’s funny, because in software stuff people sometimes ask these very long setup questions, and halfway through I’m already thinking about the bug or the architecture issue, and then I have to remind myself to listen to the rest because there might be some detail that changes everything.\n\nSo, I guess maybe occasionally? But I haven’t had people complain about that specifically.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself feeling overwhelmed by your emotions?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way. “Overwhelmed by emotions” makes me think of, like, suddenly crying or exploding or something, and that’s not really how I think of myself.\n\nI do get overwhelmed in general, but it’s usually more like there are too many inputs and I sort of mentally jam up. Emotionally… I don’t know. I can get frustrated, sure, especially when I feel like I’m behind, but I’m not sure if that’s an emotion thing or just a normal reaction.\n\nSorry, I’m kind of answering a different question. I guess my honest answer is: I haven’t really noticed that as a major pattern.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any times when you acted on impulse without fully thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a clean example, and nothing obvious is coming to mind.\n\nI mean, “impulse” makes me think of big dramatic things, like quitting a job on the spot or buying a car without checking your bank account, and I’m not really that person. I’m more likely to overthink things, honestly.\n\nThere are probably small moments where I respond too quickly to something or agree to something before I’ve really looked at my schedule, but then I’m not sure if that counts as impulse or just being distracted in the moment. Like with software, sometimes you make a change thinking, “Oh, this is definitely the issue,” and then later realize there was some edge case you didn’t consider—but that’s not really a life consequence thing, it’s more just how debugging goes.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t have a strong example. I haven’t noticed myself acting impulsively in a way that caused major problems.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience difficulties with focus, restlessness, or impulsivity as a child, aside from what you mentioned about daydreaming and losing folders?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Looking back, it wasn’t just later on. In grade school there were comments like, you know, “bright kid, but daydreams,” that kind of thing.\n\nI also remember finishing assignments and then somehow not turning them in, which sounds ridiculous now because the hard part was already done. And homework folders—I lost those a lot, long before I was a teenager. I guess at the time it was treated as me being careless or not organized, but when I look back, that pattern was already there pretty early.\n\nDoctor: Do you often avoid or dislike tasks that require sustained mental effort?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I don’t think I avoid “mental effort” in the sense that I’m lazy about thinking—my job is basically thinking all day, or at least pretending to while staring at logs and wondering why something broke.\n\nBut sustained effort… yeah, maybe that depends on the task. If it’s something where I can get into it, I can stay with it. If it’s something kind of open-ended or tedious, I’ll sometimes circle around it for a while before actually starting. Though I’m realizing I’m talking more generally than answering your question.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t have a clean yes-or-no. I know some tasks feel harder to start and stick with, but I’m not sure I’d say I often avoid them across the board.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to follow through on instructions or finish tasks, even when you're interested in them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give a clean answer to that. “Follow through” can mean a few different things, right? Like, if someone gives me a set of instructions at work, I might do the main thing and then realize later there was some smaller detail buried in there that I didn’t account for—but that also happens because software instructions are often, you know, half a sentence in a ticket written by someone who assumes you already know the entire backstory.\n\nFinishing tasks I’m interested in… I mean, sometimes I do finish them, especially if there’s a clear endpoint. But if it sort of sprawls, or if the “done” part is fuzzy, then I can drift into improving some side piece instead. Actually that reminds me of when you start cleaning one room and end up reorganizing a drawer, except I’m not saying I’m constantly doing that, it’s just the kind of thing I mean.\n\nSo… I guess I’ve noticed it in some situations, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s consistently hard, or if it’s just normal task-management stuff. I’d probably need you to define what you mean by “hard to follow through.”\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself doing things on impulse that you later regret?\n\nPatient: Hmm. I don’t know. I wouldn’t say that’s a big pattern for me, at least not in the dramatic sense.\n\nI mean, everybody does little things, right? Like buying some gadget because it seemed useful at midnight and then it sits in a drawer next to three old cables you can’t identify. Actually my house has this whole box of cables that I’m convinced will be important someday, which is probably ridiculous.\n\nBut in terms of doing impulsive things and then really regretting them… I’m not sure. Nothing obvious comes to mind. I’d probably say not often, unless you mean smaller everyday stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers in social conversations?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, I might jump in if I think I know where someone is going, but I feel like that’s kind of normal conversation too, depending on the person.\n\nActually, with software people, conversations are weird because everyone interrupts everyone on calls, and then there’s that delay where two people start talking and both stop, and then both start again. So it’s hard to tell what’s me versus just… Zoom being awful.\n\nBut socially, like with friends or family, I’m not aware of constantly blurting things out. I may have done it sometimes, sure, but I don’t think it’s something people complain about. At least not that I can think of.\n\nDoctor: Do you often do things without considering the potential consequences?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I usually do big things without thinking about what might happen. Like, I’m not the kind of person who suddenly quits a job or buys a motorcycle or whatever without thinking it through.\n\nSmall things, maybe? But then I start wondering what counts as “considering consequences.” Like, if I reply to an email too quickly and later think, “Eh, I should’ve phrased that differently,” is that the same thing? Or is it more like dangerous decisions? Because those feel pretty different.\n\nI guess I’d say I haven’t noticed it as a major pattern. But I may be defining it too narrowly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been unable to fulfill responsibilities at work or in your personal life due to your difficulties with focus, organization, or impulsivity?\n\nPatient: Yeah… unfortunately, yes. That’s probably the part that’s gotten harder to brush off.\n\nAt work especially, it’s started to affect how people see me. I missed a checkpoint for a patent filing, which sounds like this very official thing—and it is—but at the time it was just one of those “I’ll get to that after I finish this other thread” situations. Then the thread becomes three threads, and suddenly the deadline isn’t theoretical anymore.\n\nAnd there was also this architecture review that went badly. Not because I didn’t know the material, exactly, but it was a difficult one, lots of moving pieces, and I wasn’t organized enough going into it. Afterward I got the sense that some collaborators were questioning whether I should be leading the larger project. That… stung. I mean, I’ve always thought of myself as someone who can handle complex technical stuff, so having it come across like I’m unreliable is pretty uncomfortable.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

Yeah, mainly in daily life through work, I guess. Social relationships… I’m less sure how much it shows up there. But work, yes.

It’s started to damage my reputation a bit, which is weird to say out loud because I’ve always been the “technical person,” you know? Like, if there’s a hard systems problem, I can usually be useful.
