I can talk about the architecture for hours if we’re at a whiteboard, or—sorry, this is a tangent—but I actually like those deep technical debates when they’re contained. But when it’s formal and there are deadlines and everyone’s depending on me to pull it together, that’s where it starts falling apart. So yes, it’s interfering.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty with focus and restlessness, or did these issues start more recently?\n\nPatient: I think it goes way back, honestly. It’s not just a recent work-stress thing.\n\nLooking back, even in grade school, teachers used to say I was bright but that I daydreamed a lot. I’d finish assignments and then somehow not turn them in, or I’d lose the folder with the homework in it. Which is funny—well, not funny, but I remember having these color-coded folders at one point because my mom was trying to help me keep track of things, and I’d still lose the one I needed.\n\nSo yeah, this was definitely happening long before I was a teenager. It’s just that now the consequences are bigger.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, actually. I don’t think of myself as especially impulsive in the big, dramatic sense.\n\nI mean, I can make quick decisions sometimes, but… in software you sort of have to, or you end up in endless planning meetings, and then someone brings up edge cases from a product line that doesn’t even exist anymore—sorry, that’s probably not what you’re asking.\n\nIf you mean like doing risky things without caring what happens, I don’t really notice that as a pattern. I’d have to think about it more.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I don’t think of myself as someone who cuts people off all the time, but conversations at work can get kind of fast, especially in technical discussions, and sometimes people are half-answering a question while I’m already thinking of the next piece of it.\n\nBut whether that’s “interrupting” in the way you mean… I don’t know. I’d probably have to ask other people. I’m aware I can jump in when I’m excited about an idea, but I don’t know how often that comes across as rude or disruptive.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble keeping track of tasks, appointments, or deadlines?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s actually probably the biggest thing right now.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in is that I feel like I can’t organize all the moving parts anymore. I’ll have patent notes in one place, workshop prep half-started somewhere else, gallery deadlines—well, the gallery thing has its own email thread, and then team decisions are buried in Slack or meeting notes—and I end up just starting whatever feels loudest or most urgent in the moment instead of what actually matters.\n\nSo it’s not that I don’t care about deadlines. It’s more like everything is competing for attention and I can’t reliably sort it into a sane order.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with organization and focus affecting your personal relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, outside of work there’s less of a formal “deadline” structure, so it’s harder for me to tell what counts as organization versus just normal life messiness.\n\nI don’t think people are, like, sitting me down and saying, “You’re impossible to deal with.” But I can imagine it probably shows up in little ways. Plans get discussed over text, then there’s a calendar invite, then someone mentions a different time in passing, and I’ll think I know what’s happening until I realize I’m remembering an older version of the plan.\n\nBut whether that’s affecting relationships in a major way… I honestly don’t know. I haven’t really tracked it like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you blurt out answers before questions have been completed?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’m usually blurting out answers before someone finishes, at least not in a way I’ve noticed.\n\nSometimes if I think I know where a question is going, especially in a meeting, I might start forming the answer before they’re done, but whether I actually say it out loud too early… I don’t know. It’s funny, because in software stuff people sometimes ask these very long setup questions, and halfway through I’m already thinking about the bug or the architecture issue, and then I have to remind myself to listen to the rest because there might be some detail that changes everything.\n\nSo, I guess maybe occasionally? But I haven’t had people complain about that specifically.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself feeling overwhelmed by your emotions?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way. “Overwhelmed by emotions” makes me think of, like, suddenly crying or exploding or something, and that’s not really how I think of myself.\n\nI do get overwhelmed in general, but it’s usually more like there are too many inputs and I sort of mentally jam up. Emotionally… I don’t know. I can get frustrated, sure, especially when I feel like I’m behind, but I’m not sure if that’s an emotion thing or just a normal reaction.\n\nSorry, I’m kind of answering a different question. I guess my honest answer is: I haven’t really noticed that as a major pattern.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any times when you acted on impulse without fully thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a clean example, and nothing obvious is coming to mind.\n\nI mean, “impulse” makes me think of big dramatic things, like quitting a job on the spot or buying a car without checking your bank account, and I’m not really that person. I’m more likely to overthink things, honestly.\n\nThere are probably small moments where I respond too quickly to something or agree to something before I’ve really looked at my schedule, but then I’m not sure if that counts as impulse or just being distracted in the moment. Like with software, sometimes you make a change thinking, “Oh, this is definitely the issue,” and then later realize there was some edge case you didn’t consider—but that’s not really a life consequence thing, it’s more just how debugging goes.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t have a strong example. I haven’t noticed myself acting impulsively in a way that caused major problems.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience difficulties with focus, restlessness, or impulsivity as a child, aside from what you mentioned about daydreaming and losing folders?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Looking back, it wasn’t just later on. In grade school there were comments like, you know, “bright kid, but daydreams,” that kind of thing.\n\nI also remember finishing assignments and then somehow not turning them in, which sounds ridiculous now because the hard part was already done. And homework folders—I lost those a lot, long before I was a teenager. I guess at the time it was treated as me being careless or not organized, but when I look back, that pattern was already there pretty early.\n\nDoctor: Do you often avoid or dislike tasks that require sustained mental effort?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I don’t think I avoid “mental effort” in the sense that I’m lazy about thinking—my job is basically thinking all day, or at least pretending to while staring at logs and wondering why something broke.\n\nBut sustained effort… yeah, maybe that depends on the task. If it’s something where I can get into it, I can stay with it. If it’s something kind of open-ended or tedious, I’ll sometimes circle around it for a while before actually starting. Though I’m realizing I’m talking more generally than answering your question.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t have a clean yes-or-no. I know some tasks feel harder to start and stick with, but I’m not sure I’d say I often avoid them across the board.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to follow through on instructions or finish tasks, even when you're interested in them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give a clean answer to that. “Follow through” can mean a few different things, right? Like, if someone gives me a set of instructions at work, I might do the main thing and then realize later there was some smaller detail buried in there that I didn’t account for—but that also happens because software instructions are often, you know, half a sentence in a ticket written by someone who assumes you already know the entire backstory.\n\nFinishing tasks I’m interested in… I mean, sometimes I do finish them, especially if there’s a clear endpoint. But if it sort of sprawls, or if the “done” part is fuzzy, then I can drift into improving some side piece instead. Actually that reminds me of when you start cleaning one room and end up reorganizing a drawer, except I’m not saying I’m constantly doing that, it’s just the kind of thing I mean.\n\nSo… I guess I’ve noticed it in some situations, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s consistently hard, or if it’s just normal task-management stuff. I’d probably need you to define what you mean by “hard to follow through.”\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself doing things on impulse that you later regret?\n\nPatient: Hmm. I don’t know. I wouldn’t say that’s a big pattern for me, at least not in the dramatic sense.\n\nI mean, everybody does little things, right? Like buying some gadget because it seemed useful at midnight and then it sits in a drawer next to three old cables you can’t identify. Actually my house has this whole box of cables that I’m convinced will be important someday, which is probably ridiculous.\n\nBut in terms of doing impulsive things and then really regretting them… I’m not sure. Nothing obvious comes to mind. I’d probably say not often, unless you mean smaller everyday stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers in social conversations?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, I might jump in if I think I know where someone is going, but I feel like that’s kind of normal conversation too, depending on the person.\n\nActually, with software people, conversations are weird because everyone interrupts everyone on calls, and then there’s that delay where two people start talking and both stop, and then both start again. So it’s hard to tell what’s me versus just… Zoom being awful.\n\nBut socially, like with friends or family, I’m not aware of constantly blurting things out. I may have done it sometimes, sure, but I don’t think it’s something people complain about. At least not that I can think of.\n\nDoctor: Do you often do things without considering the potential consequences?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I usually do big things without thinking about what might happen. Like, I’m not the kind of person who suddenly quits a job or buys a motorcycle or whatever without thinking it through.\n\nSmall things, maybe? But then I start wondering what counts as “considering consequences.” Like, if I reply to an email too quickly and later think, “Eh, I should’ve phrased that differently,” is that the same thing? Or is it more like dangerous decisions? Because those feel pretty different.\n\nI guess I’d say I haven’t noticed it as a major pattern. But I may be defining it too narrowly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been unable to fulfill responsibilities at work or in your personal life due to your difficulties with focus, organization, or impulsivity?\n\nPatient: Yeah… unfortunately, yes. That’s probably the part that’s gotten harder to brush off.\n\nAt work especially, it’s started to affect how people see me. I missed a checkpoint for a patent filing, which sounds like this very official thing—and it is—but at the time it was just one of those “I’ll get to that after I finish this other thread” situations. Then the thread becomes three threads, and suddenly the deadline isn’t theoretical anymore.\n\nAnd there was also this architecture review that went badly. Not because I didn’t know the material, exactly, but it was a difficult one, lots of moving pieces, and I wasn’t organized enough going into it. Afterward I got the sense that some collaborators were questioning whether I should be leading the larger project. That… stung. I mean, I’ve always thought of myself as someone who can handle complex technical stuff, so having it come across like I’m unreliable is pretty uncomfortable.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant difficulties in your social relationships or daily life due to your symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity?\n\nPatient: Yeah, mainly in daily life through work, I guess. Social relationships… I’m less sure how much it shows up there. But work, yes.\n\nIt’s started to damage my reputation a bit, which is weird to say out loud because I’ve always been the “technical person,” you know? Like, if there’s a hard systems problem, I can usually be useful. But then I missed that patent filing checkpoint, and that’s the kind of thing people don’t really forget because it’s tied to process and deadlines and official-looking documents.\n\nAnd then the architecture review… that one still bothers me. It was a difficult review, and I wasn’t organized enough going in, so it landed badly. After that, I got the sense that some collaborators were wondering if I should really be leading the larger project. Which is embarrassing, because it’s not that I don’t care or don’t understand the work. It just ends up looking that way."
